It is because of the pole, because of the axle, the wheels, the body, the flag-staff, the yoke, the reins and because of the goad that ‘chariot’ exists as a mere designation. Nāgasena: It is good, sire, that you understand a chariot. Even so is it for me, sire, because of the hairs of the head … because of the urine, because of material form … because of consciousness, that ‘Nāgasena’ exists as a mere designation. However, in the ultimate sense there is no person as such that is found."

Nāgasena: Is the flame of the first watch of the night the same as the flame of the middle watch? King Milinda: No, revered sir. Nāgasena: Is the flame of the middle watch the same as the flame of the third watch? King Milinda: No, revered sir. Nāgasena: Is it then, sire, that the lamp in the first watch was one thing, the lamp in the middle watch another, and the lamp in the third watch still another? King Milinda: No, revered sir, it was burning all through the night in dependence on itself. Nāgasena: Even so, sire, a continuity of phenomena is linked together. It uprises as one and ceases as another, linked together as though there were no before and no after. Hence it is neither the same nor another, but the subsequent consciousness is reckoned (together with) the preceding consciousness."

King Milinda: Yes, revered sir, he would deserve punishment. Nāgasena: For what reason? King Milinda: Whatever he may say, the mangoes he stole resulted from the mangoes which, he admits, the other man planted. King Milinda: If, revered sir, this mind-and-matter itself does not take rebirth would one not be freed from evil deeds? Nāgasena: If, sire, it did not take rebirth one would be freed from evil deeds. But because, sire, it takes rebirth one is not freed from evil deeds. ... Suppose, sire, some man were to steal another man’s mangoes and the owner of the mangoes were to seize him and bring him before the king... and he were to say, ‘Your majesty, I did not steal his mangoes; the mangoes he planted were different from these that I stole; I do not deserve punishment.’ Would he, sire, not deserve punishment? King Milinda: Yes, revered sir, he would deserve punishment. Nāgasena: For what reason? King Milinda: Whatever he may say, the mangoes he stole resulted from the mangoes which, he admits, the other man planted. Nāgasena: Even so, sire, by means of this mind-and-matter one does a good or bad deed and, because of this deed, another mind-and-matter takes rebirth; therefore, one is not freed from evil deeds."

Nāgasena: Then, sire, does the earlier water instruct the later, saying ‘You run away by the same course as I do,’ or does the later water instruct the earlier, saying ‘I will also run away by the same course as you will take’? King Milinda: No, revered sir, there is no conversation between them; they run away because there is a slope. Nāgasena: Even so, sire, it is because of tendency that where eye-consciousness arises, mind-consciousness arises there too."

King Milinda: But if the offerings made by such givers benefit certain of the departed... then if a man who destroys living creatures... were to dedicate it to the departed... would it then be transferred to them? Nāgasena: No, sire. King Milinda: Revered Nāgasena, what is the cause, what is the reason that a good deed can accrue to them and not an evil one? Nāgasena: An evil deed is slight, sire, a good deed is abundant. ... Make a simile. ... It is as, sire, one small drop of water might fall on the earth, yet would that drop of water, sire, spread out over ten or twelve leagues? King Milinda: No, revered sir... Nāgasena: Even so, sire, an evil deed is slight... it affects only the doer... But as, sire, a mighty and great cloud were to pour down rain satisfying the earth’s surface, yet would the water from that great cloud, spread out all round? King Milinda: Certainly, revered sir. Nāgasena: Even so, sire, a good deed is abundant; because of its abundance one is able to share it even with devas and mankind."
